Unit-PAEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY
Chapter-Congenital Heart Diseases  (CHD) and Genetic Syndromes
Topic-Clinical Features and Diagnosis
An ill 16-day-old baby girl is brought to the emergency. On examination, pallor and dyspnoea with a respiratory rate of 85/min are noted. Her heart rate is 200 bpm, heart sounds are distant, and a gallop is heard. X-ray showed cardiomegaly. An echocardiogram shows dilated ventricles and dilation of the left atrium. An ECG shows ventricular depolarisation complexes that have low voltage. What is the most likely diagnosis?|CHF|Glycogen Storage Disease|Pericarditis|Aberrant left coronary artery arising from the pulmonary artery|CHF|The clinical presentation of pallor, dyspnoea, gallop rhythm, and cardiomegaly in a neonate suggests congestive heart failure (CHF), often secondary to congenital heart defects.|Medium|Clinical|

A newborn girl is evaluated in the neonatal intensive care unit due to congenital anomalies noted at birth. The infant was born through a caesarean section at 37 weeks of gestation to a 26-year-old primigravida who declined prenatal testing. The oral mucosa is pink, but the hands and feet are mildly cyanotic. Apgar scores were 6 and 8 at 1 and 5 minutes, respectively. Her temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), blood pressure is 97/65 mmHg, heart rate is 120/min, and respiratory rate is 24/min. Examination reveals upper limb defects, including shortening of the forearms and triphalangeal thumbs. Cardiac auscultation reveals a systolic murmur most prominent at the left 2nd intercostal space and fixed splitting of S2. The pulse oximetry shows an oxygen saturation of 95%. X-ray reveals upper limb defects, including shortening of the radius and triphalangeal thumbs. Which of the following is the most common cardiac defect in this neonate?|Atrial septal defect (ASD)|Ventricular septal defect (VSD)|Transposition of great arteries (TGA)|Total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (TAPVC)|Atrial septal defect (ASD)|Holt-Oram syndrome is an autosomal dominant disorder affecting limb and heart development. ASD (commonly secundum type) is the most common cardiac anomaly in these patients.|Medium|Clinical|

Which congenital heart disease has the LEAST chance of heart failure in infants?|TOF|VSD|TAPVC|ASD|TOF|Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF) typically presents with cyanosis rather than heart failure in infants due to right-to-left shunting. Heart failure is more common in left-to-right shunting lesions like VSD or TAPVC.|Medium|General|

Prevalence of congenital heart disease is?|0.8%|1-2%|2-6%|10-12%|0.8%|Congenital heart disease affects approximately 0.8% of live births worldwide, though it may vary slightly by region.|Easy|General|

Most common cardiac lesion in Down’s syndrome is?|Atrioventricular septal defect|VSD|Coarctation of aorta|Transposition of great vessels|Atrioventricular septal defect|Atrioventricular septal defect is the most common congenital heart defect associated with Down’s syndrome due to abnormal endocardial cushion development.|Easy|Clinical|

Recurrent respiratory tract infection occurs in all of the following except?|VSD|TOF|TGA|TAPVC|TOF|TOF primarily causes cyanosis rather than recurrent respiratory tract infections, which are more common in left-to-right shunting defects like VSD, TAPVC, or TGA.|Medium|General|

A six-week-old infant is brought with complaints of feeding difficulty, forehead sweating, fast breathing, tachycardia, tachypnea, and hepatomegaly. CXR shows cardiomegaly. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Bacterial endocarditis|CCF|Cyanotic spell|Cor pulmonale|CCF|Congestive cardiac failure (CCF) in infants is often due to congenital heart diseases causing left-to-right shunts like VSD or PDA, leading to pulmonary congestion.|Medium|Clinical|

Most common congenital heart disease is?|ASD|VSD|PDA|TOF|VSD|Ventricular septal defect (VSD) is the most common congenital heart defect, accounting for approximately 25–30% of all cases.|Easy|General|

The congenital heart disease most commonly associated with preexcitation syndrome is?|Double outlet RV|Taussig-Bing anomaly|Ebstein’s anomaly|Truncus arteriosus|Ebstein’s anomaly|Ebstein’s anomaly is associated with WPW (preexcitation) syndrome due to accessory conduction pathways.|Medium|General|

Topic-Syndromic Associations
A newborn girl is evaluated in neonatal intensive care due to congenital anomalies noted at birth. The infant was born through a caesarean section at the 37th week of pregnancy to a 26-year-old woman, primigravida, who declined prenatal testing. The oral mucosa is pink, but the hands and feet are mildly cyanotic. Apgar scores were 6 and 8 at 1 and 5 minutes, respectively. Examination reveals upper-limb defects, including shortening of forearms and triphalangeal thumbs. Cardiac auscultation reveals a systolic murmur most prominent at the left 2nd intercostal space and fixed splitting of S2. The pulse oximetry shows an oxygen saturation of 95%. X-ray reveals upper limb defects, including shortening of the radius and triphalangeal thumbs. Which of the following is the most common cardiac defect in this neonate?|Atrial septal defect (ASD)|Ventricular septal defect (VSD)|Transposition of great arteries (TGA)|Total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (TAPVC)|Atrial septal defect (ASD)|The Holt-Oram syndrome is an autosomal dominant disorder associated with ASD and upper limb anomalies (triphalangeal thumb, radial defects). ASD presents with a systolic murmur at the left 2nd intercostal space and fixed splitting of S2.|Medium|Clinical|

The most common congenital heart disease associated with thymic hypoplasia is?|PS|Truncus arteriosus|PDA|TOF|Truncus arteriosus|Thymic hypoplasia (DiGeorge syndrome) is commonly associated with truncus arteriosus due to abnormal development of the third and fourth pharyngeal arches.|Medium|General|

Coarctation of the aorta is common in which syndrome?|Down’s syndrome|Noonan’s syndrome|Turner’s syndrome|Klinefelter’s syndrome|Turner’s syndrome|Coarctation of the aorta is a common cardiac defect in Turner’s syndrome, along with bicuspid aortic valve.|Easy|General|

The congenital heart disease associated with lithium intake during pregnancy?|TOF|TGA|ECD|Ebstein’s anomaly|Ebstein’s anomaly|Maternal lithium use during pregnancy is strongly associated with Ebstein’s anomaly, characterized by apical displacement of the tricuspid valve.|Medium|General|

Topic-Imaging in CHD
Right-sided aortic arch is commonly associated with?|Truncus arteriosus|TAPVC|Endocardial cushion defect|PDA|Truncus arteriosus|Right-sided aortic arch is often seen in truncus arteriosus and other conotruncal abnormalities like TOF.|Medium|General|

The following CXR appearance is seen in which condition?|TOF|TGA|TAPVC|Truncus arteriosus|TGA|TGA shows an "egg-on-a-string" appearance on chest X-ray due to a narrow mediastinum and cardiomegaly.|Medium|Clinical|

Box-shaped heart is seen in?|TOF|TGA|Ebstein’s anomaly|Tricuspid atresia|Ebstein’s anomaly|Ebstein’s anomaly is characterized by a "box-shaped" heart on CXR due to massive right atrial enlargement.|Medium|General|

Identify the disease associated with this CXR:|TOF|TGA|TAPVC|Truncus arteriosus|TAPVC|CXR findings in total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (TAPVC) often show a "snowman" or "figure-of-8" appearance due to enlarged veins and dilated right atrium.|Medium|Clinical|

The only cyanotic disease showing LV hypertrophy on CXR is:|Tricuspid atresia|Ebstein’s anomaly|Truncus arteriosus|PDA with Eisenmenger|Tricuspid atresia|Tricuspid atresia causes left ventricular hypertrophy due to underdevelopment of the right ventricle and increased systemic flow via the left ventricle.|Medium|Clinical|

Topic-Rare and Special CHDs

NADAS criteria is useful for?|Assessment of congenital heart disease in adults|Assessment of congenital heart disease in children|Assessment of dehydration in both children and adults|Assessment of congenital heart disease only in pregnancy|Assessment of congenital heart disease in children|NADAS criteria help evaluate and categorize congenital heart defects in pediatric populations.|Medium|General|

The congenital heart disease which is not ductal dependent?|Truncus arteriosus|TGA with intact ventricular septum|Hypoplastic left heart syndrome|Critical pulmonary stenosis|Truncus arteriosus|Truncus arteriosus is not ductal-dependent as there is a single outflow tract supplying both systemic and pulmonary circulations.|Medium|General|

Topic-Cyanotic CHD with Pulmonary Oligemia
Which of the following congenital heart diseases has complete mixing of blood on the left and right side?|Total anomalous pulmonary venous connection|Ventricular septal defect|Transposition of great arteries|Patent Ductus Arteriosus|Total anomalous pulmonary venous connection|Total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (TAPVC) results in complete mixing of oxygenated and deoxygenated blood, leading to cyanosis.|Tough|Clinical|

An 8-year-old girl has been brought to the hospital due to a fever associated with pharyngitis for the past few days. Her electrocardiogram is ordered, which reveals the presence of a prolonged PR interval. Because of acute rheumatic fever, the patient was started on penicillin. The patient developed a severe rash and breathing problems after being treated. Which of the following is the drug of choice for rheumatic fever prophylaxis in penicillin-allergic patients?|Erythromycin|Cephalexin|Vancomycin|Gentamicin|Erythromycin|Erythromycin is the first-line prophylactic treatment for acute rheumatic fever in patients allergic to penicillin.|Medium|Clinical|

What is the most common congenital heart disease in a neonate who has bilateral cataracts and was born to a mother with a history of fever during the first trimester of pregnancy?|Ventricular septal defect|Atrial septal defect|Patent ductus arteriosus|Tetralogy of Fallot|Patent ductus arteriosus|Congenital rubella syndrome leads to PDA, cataracts, and sensorineural deafness as part of the classic triad.|Medium|Clinical|

A one-year-old child was brought to the emergency department with complaints of breathlessness on exertion which improved in a squatting position. On examination, the child had clubbing and cyanosis. ECG and Chest X-ray were advised and showed RVH pattern and a boot-shaped heart respectively. Which of the following is not an auscultatory finding in this condition?|Ejection systolic murmur in the pulmonary area|Single S2|Continuous murmur|Diastolic murmur in the tricuspid area|Continuous murmur|Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF) presents with an ejection systolic murmur in the pulmonary area, a single S2, and a boot-shaped heart on X-ray. A continuous murmur is not characteristic of TOF and is typically seen in PDA.|Medium|Clinical|

A cyanotic newborn is suspected of having CHD. She has a left ventricular impulse, with holosystolic murmur audible at the left sternal border, single S2, decreased pulmonary blood flow, oligemic lung fields, and ECG shows left axis deviation. The most probable diagnosis is?|TOF|TGA|Tricuspid atresia|TAPVC|Tricuspid atresia|Tricuspid atresia is characterized by left axis deviation, single S2, and decreased pulmonary blood flow (oligemia). The left ventricle becomes dominant due to right ventricular underdevelopment.|Medium|Clinical|

Chapter-Imaging and Follow-Up
Topic-X-Ray and Spontaneous Closure
A one-year-old child was brought to the emergency department with complaints of breathlessness on exertion which improved in a squatting position. On examination, the child had clubbing and cyanosis. ECG and Chest X-ray were advised and showed RVH pattern and a boot-shaped heart respectively. Which of the following is not an auscultatory finding in this condition?|Ejection systolic murmur in the pulmonary area|Single S2|Continuous murmur|Diastolic murmur in the tricuspid area|Continuous murmur|Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF) is characterized by cyanosis, right ventricular hypertrophy (RVH), and a boot-shaped heart on X-ray. A continuous murmur is not an auscultatory finding in TOF.|Medium|Clinical|

Which of the following features on X-ray chest can differentiate an atrial septal defect (ASD) and a ventricular septal defect (VSD)?|Enlarged left atrium|Pulmonary plethora|Enlarged pulmonary artery|Enlarged aorta|Enlarged left atrium|Enlarged left atrium is a hallmark of VSD due to volume overload, whereas it is not typically seen in ASD.|Medium|General|

A 2-year-old child was diagnosed with VSD with intermittent CCF and recurrent pneumonias. But the child was lost to follow-up and was seen 5 years later. The parents reported spontaneous resolution of CCF in the child since past few weeks. What is your interpretation?|Parents are lying|Spontaneous closure of a muscular VSD|Restriction of L → R shunt by restrictive VSD|Impending Eisenmenger syndrome|Impending Eisenmenger syndrome|The resolution of symptoms suggests a decrease in left-to-right shunting due to pulmonary hypertension and the onset of Eisenmenger syndrome.|Medium|Clinical|

Unit-PAEDIATRIC CARDIOLOGY
Chapter-Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF)
Topic-Features and Complications
A 3-year-old child presents to the emergency department due to sudden onset of cyanosis, increased respiratory effort, and agitation following a crying episode. On examination, the child is noted to have a loud systolic murmur best heard at the left upper sternal border. The mother says the child experiences these symptoms frequently and temporarily improves with knee-to-chest positioning. Laboratory findings are significant for polycythemia. A chest X-ray shows a boot-shaped heart. Which of the following is the best treatment option for this child's condition?|Lisinopril|Propranolol|Hydrochlorothiazide|Albuterol|Propranolol|TOF spells (hypercyanotic episodes) are treated with propranolol, a beta-blocker that reduces infundibular spasm and improves right ventricular outflow.|Medium|Clinical|

All are true about tetralogy of Fallot except?|Right-sided aortic arch seen in 85% cases|Associated with CATCH-22 syndrome|Persistence of a left superior vena cava is seen in|Multiple ventricular septal defects may be seen|Right-sided aortic arch seen in 85% cases|Right-sided aortic arch is seen in approximately 25% of TOF cases, not 85%. TOF is associated with CATCH-22 syndrome and may have persistent left superior vena cava or multiple VSDs.|Medium|General|

An infant had repeated episodes of bluish discoloration of lips, which were relieved by squatting position. Chest X-ray is shown below. What is the likely diagnosis?|Ostium primum ASD|VSD with severe pneumonia|TGA|TOF|TOF|Cyanotic spells relieved by squatting and characteristic CXR findings (boot-shaped heart) suggest TOF.|Medium|Clinical|

All of the following are used for the treatment of cyanotic spells except:|Phenylephrine|Calcium chloride|Sodium bicarbonate|Propranolol|Calcium chloride|Calcium chloride is not used for cyanotic spells. Treatment includes phenylephrine (increases afterload), sodium bicarbonate (corrects acidosis), and propranolol (reduces infundibular spasm).|Medium|Clinical|

The most consistent feature suggestive of a child with TOF being in a cyanotic spell is:|SpO2 <85%|Disappearance of pulmonary ejection systolic murmur|Syncope in the child|Hyperventilation|Disappearance of pulmonary ejection systolic murmur|In a cyanotic spell, infundibular spasm decreases pulmonary blood flow, causing the pulmonary ejection systolic murmur to disappear.|Medium|Clinical|

Topic-Neurological Sequelae

What is the most probable diagnosis?|Paradoxical emboli to CNS|Cyanotic spell|Brain abscess|Meningitis|Brain abscess|Brain abscess is a recognized complication of TOF due to polycythemia and right-to-left shunting, allowing septic emboli to bypass the lungs.|Medium|Clinical|

Chapter-Eisenmenger Syndrome
Topic-Features
A 7-month-old infant is brought by his parents to the hospital due to severe fatigue, poor feeding, and irritability. The mother also expresses concern that her baby has started to appear "dusky" in recent times. Medical records show that the infant was previously hospitalized for pneumonia and was referred to a pediatric cardiologist. Physical examination is notable for central cyanosis, tachypnea, tachycardia, and hepatomegaly. Cardiac auscultation reveals a single second heart sound and a holosystolic murmur at the left lower sternal border. Echocardiography shows the absence of a particular valve with the left ventricle larger than the right. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Tricuspid atresia|Eisenmenger’s Syndrome|Tetralogy of Fallot|Total Anomalous Pulmonary Venous Return (TAPVR)|Tricuspid atresia|The combination of cyanosis, holosystolic murmur, and single S2 in an infant strongly suggests tricuspid atresia, a condition characterized by the absence of the tricuspid valve.|Tough|Clinical|

The Pediatrics HOD started a lecture on blood circulation by discussing pressures in different adult heart chambers. She then questioned a medical student about the right ventricular pressure in the adult heart. What is the systolic pressure in the right ventricle in adult circulation?|25 mmHg|0-6 mmHg|6-10 mmHg|80-120 mmHg|25 mmHg|In adult circulation, the systolic pressure of the right ventricle is around 25 mmHg, while the diastolic pressure is near 0-6 mmHg.|Easy|General|

A 10-year-old male child is brought to the hospital with complaints of cyanosis, fatigue, and dyspnea. He has associated chest pain and tightness. The patient's history reveals the presence of frequent chest infections in childhood. The mother gives a history of some diagnosed congenital heart defect which was not treated. On further examination, there is equal cyanosis in both fingers and toes. A provisional diagnosis of Eisenmenger syndrome is made. Eisenmenger syndrome is not seen in which of the following conditions?|Atrial septal defect (ASD)|Ventricular septal defect (VSD)|Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF)|Patent ductus arteriosus (PDA)|Tetralogy of Fallot (TOF)|Eisenmenger syndrome occurs due to left-to-right shunting (seen in ASD, VSD, and PDA) that eventually reverses to right-to-left due to pulmonary hypertension. TOF, being a primary right-to-left shunt, does not cause Eisenmenger syndrome.|Medium|General|

Eisenmenger syndrome is characterized by all except:|Return of left ventricle & right ventricle to normal size|Right ventricular hypertrophy|Pruning of peripheral pulmonary arteries|Dilatation of central pulmonary artery|Return of left ventricle & right ventricle to normal size|Eisenmenger syndrome involves irreversible pulmonary hypertension, right ventricular hypertrophy, pruning of peripheral arteries, and central pulmonary artery dilation, but the ventricles do not return to normal size.|Medium|General|

Congestive heart failure in children is first diagnosed by:|Tachycardia and tender hepatomegaly|Increased JVP|Increased JVP + pedal edema|Hypotension|Tachycardia and tender hepatomegaly|Tachycardia and tender hepatomegaly are early signs of congestive heart failure in children. True about a 1-year-old child with PDA is?|Symptoms similar to aorto-pulmonary window|Chances of spontaneous closure high|Indomethacin may help in closure|Endocarditis is rare|Symptoms similar to aorto-pulmonary window|A large PDA can cause symptoms similar to aorto-pulmonary window due to left-to-right shunting. A term 10-week-old male baby was brought to the clinic due to inadequate weight gain. On examination by the Pediatrician, tachypnea and tachycardia were present. On auscultation, a continuous machinery murmur was found. Chest X-ray was advised, and the report showed cardiomegaly with LV apex prominence. What is the drug of choice for this condition in preterm neonates?|Indomethacin|Aspirin|Digoxin|Furosemide|Indomethacin|Patent Ductus Arteriosus (PDA) is closed pharmacologically in preterm neonates using Indomethacin or Ibuprofen, which inhibit prostaglandin synthesis.|Easy|Clinical|

A preterm baby with large PDA and CCF has associated renal failure, thrombocytopenia, IVH, and NNEC. The most appropriate modality of PDA closure would be?|Indomethacin 0.2 mg/kg 3 doses at the 12th hourly intervals IV/PO|Brufen – 10 mg/kg 3 doses at 24-hour interval|Bedside ligation of PDA|PGE infusion|Bedside ligation of PDA|In cases where medical management with indomethacin or brufen is contraindicated due to associated complications (e.g., NEC, IVH), surgical ligation is the preferred treatment.|Medium|Clinical|

Drug used to keep DA open is?|PGE1|PGE2|PGI2|TXA2|PGE1|Prostaglandin E1 (PGE1) is used to maintain ductal patency in duct-dependent congenital heart diseases until definitive surgical repair is performed.|Easy|Clinical|

Chapter-Shock
Topic-Shock Management
A 2-year-old child with a large untreated boil on the right thigh comes in septic shock. He is started on IV fluids, and after giving 3 NS boluses, he develops basal crepitations, but the shock continues. He is also administered a dose of Hydrocortisone and started on Dopamine infusion. Currently, he is on Dopamine infusion @ 20 micrograms/kg/min. What is the next therapy to consider?|Start 5% Albumin infusion|Start I.V. Antibiotics|Add epinephrine infusion i.v.|Add norepinephrine infusion i.v.|Add norepinephrine infusion i.v.|Norepinephrine is the next step in managing refractory septic shock after Dopamine fails, as it effectively improves vascular tone and perfusion.|Medium|Clinical|

Unit-Paediatric Critical Care

Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology
Chapter-General Endocrinology
Topic-Preventable Disorders
A 9-year-old girl presents to the pediatric clinic with complaints of excessive thirst and urination for the past few weeks. On examination, the child has dry mucous membranes. Vital signs are within normal limits. Laboratory investigations reveal low urine specific gravity and osmolality, along with hyperosmolality of the plasma. Lesions in which of the hypothalamic nuclei are responsible for the most likely diagnosis?|Dorsomedial nuclei|Supraoptic and paraventricular nuclei|Median preoptic nuclei|Ventromedial nuclei

Supraoptic and paraventricular nuclei



The supraoptic and paraventricular nuclei of the hypothalamus produce antidiuretic hormone (ADH). Damage to these nuclei leads to central diabetes insipidus (DI), characterized by polyuria, polydipsia, and a low urine osmolality. In central DI, the kidneys fail to concentrate urine due to a lack of ADH secretion. Management includes desmopressin (ADH analog).|Medium|Clinical|

A 7-day-old male neonate is brought to the pediatric outpatient department due to constipation. He has passed only one stool since birth. There is no history of any familial disorder. Physical examination reveals a large tongue, pallor, and rough, dry skin. What is the most likely cause of this patient’s presentation?|Thyroid dysgenesis|Iodine deficiency|Pendred syndrome|Thyroid dyshormonogenesis

Thyroid dysgenesis



Congenital hypothyroidism is commonly caused by thyroid dysgenesis (ectopic or absent thyroid gland). Clinical features: Large tongue, pallor, dry skin, lethargy, constipation Umbilical hernia, hypotonia Prolonged jaundice Screening: Newborn TSH screening at 48 hours after birth. Treatment: Levothyroxine (10-15 mcg/kg/day).|Medium|Clinical|

Which of the following is most likely to develop in a baby born to a mother with tremors, anxiety, heat intolerance, inability to gain weight, and increased appetite during pregnancy?|Heart failure|Constipation|Third-degree heart block|Macrocephaly

Heart failure



The mother likely has Graves’ disease or hyperthyroidism, which can cause neonatal thyrotoxicosis due to transplacental passage of maternal TSH receptor-stimulating antibodies (TRAb). Neonatal thyrotoxicosis symptoms: Tachycardia, irritability, poor weight gain Heart failure due to increased metabolic demand Craniosynostosis due to accelerated bone maturation Management: Beta-blockers (Propranolol) + Methimazole|Medium|Clinical|

What is the most likely diagnosis for a patient with skin lesions, precocious puberty, and fibrous dysplasia of the skeletal system?|Prader-Willi syndrome|Laurence-Moon-Biedl syndrome|Cushing syndrome|McCune-Albright syndrome

McCune-Albright syndrome



McCune-Albright Syndrome (MAS) is a rare genetic disorder caused by a somatic mutation in the GNAS1 gene, leading to mosaicism. Triad of MAS: Polyostotic fibrous dysplasia (bone lesions causing fractures and deformity) Precocious puberty (due to autonomous ovarian function) Café-au-lait spots (large, irregular, "coast of Maine" borders) Treatment: Symptomatic management, bisphosphonates for fibrous dysplasia, GnRH analogs for puberty.|Medium|Clinical|

Which of the following is a preventable cause of mental retardation?|Hypothyroidism|Down’s syndrome|Cerebral palsy|All of the above|Hypothyroidism|Hypothyroidism, particularly congenital hypothyroidism, is a preventable cause of mental retardation if detected early and treated. Down's syndrome and cerebral palsy are not preventable.|Medium|General|

Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology
Chapter-Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH)
Topic-Ambiguous Genitalia
A 9-year-old male presents due to a headache for 1 week. The headache is located all over the head and is 7/10 in intensity. It is associated with a vision change. He denies fever, nausea, vomiting, aura, or neurological changes. Physical examination reveals a very tall male child with coarse facies, enlarged hands and feet, and macroglossia. X-ray of the upper limbs shows open epiphyses. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?|Gigantism|Acromegaly|Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome|Homocystinuria

Gigantism



Gigantism occurs due to excess growth hormone (GH) secretion before epiphyseal plate closure, leading to excessive height. Symptoms: Coarse facies, macroglossia, enlarged extremities, and headaches due to mass effect of pituitary adenoma. Investigations: Elevated IGF-1 levels, oral glucose tolerance test (GH remains high), and pituitary MRI. Treatment: Transsphenoidal surgery for pituitary adenoma and somatostatin analogs (octreotide).|Medium|Clinical|

If left untreated, what is the most likely future complication for an 8-month-old male diagnosed with hypothyroidism caused by iodine deficiency?|Shortening of limbs with normal stature|Short stature with normal limbs|Short limbs and short stature|Short limbs and tall stature

Short limbs and short stature



Untreated congenital hypothyroidism results in cretinism. Features of cretinism: Short stature due to growth retardation Short limbs due to delayed epiphyseal maturation Severe intellectual disability Coarse facial features, macroglossia, umbilical hernia Prevention: Early levothyroxine replacement is critical.|Medium|Clinical|

The most common cause of ambiguous genitalia in a newborn is:|21 hydroxylase deficiency|11 beta hydroxylase deficiency|17 alpha hydroxylase deficiency|3 beta hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase deficiency|21 hydroxylase deficiency|21 hydroxylase deficiency accounts for 90-95% of CAH cases, causing ambiguous genitalia due to androgen excess.|Medium|General|

Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology
Chapter-Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH)
Topic-Clinical Features
What is the most likely cause of a condition characterized by short stature, obesity, normal body proportions, micropenis, and a blood sugar level of 59 mg/dL in an 11-year-old male patient?|Malabsorption|Growth hormone deficiency|Adrenal tumor|Hypothyroidism

Growth hormone deficiency



Growth hormone deficiency (GHD) leads to short stature with normal body proportions, mild obesity, and hypoglycemia due to lack of GH-mediated gluconeogenesis. Micropenis can be seen in congenital GH deficiency. Bone age delay (skeletal age younger than actual age) is a classic feature. Treatment: GH replacement therapy.|Medium|Clinical|

A 5-month-old male presents with an abnormal TSH test showing TSH = 70mU/L. The clinician diagnoses congenital hypothyroidism. Which of the following is not a clinical feature associated with congenital hypothyroidism?|Umbilical hernia|Open posterior fontanelle|Distended abdomen|Diarrhea

Congenital hypothyroidism symptoms: Lethargy, hypotonia, large tongue Umbilical hernia, distended abdomen Prolonged jaundice Open posterior fontanelle (delayed closure) Diarrhea is not a feature; instead, constipation is common.|Easy|Clinical|

A 7-week-old neonate presents to the emergency department due to severe lethargy. The baby was recently treated for dehydration and shock. On examination, the baby has ambiguous genitalia. Which electrolyte abnormality is most likely to be present in this patient?|Hypokalemia|Hyperkalemia|Hypocalcemia|Hypercalcemia

Hyperkalemia



Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH) is a group of autosomal recessive disorders caused by enzyme defects in steroidogenesis, most commonly 21-hydroxylase deficiency. Classic salt-wasting form of CAH presents with: Ambiguous genitalia in females Hypotension, dehydration, hyperkalemia, and hyponatremia (due to aldosterone deficiency) Cortisol deficiency → Hypoglycemia, vomiting, lethargy Diagnosis: ↑17-hydroxyprogesterone levels, ACTH stimulation test Treatment: Hydrocortisone + Fludrocortisone (mineralocorticoid replacement)|Medium|Clinical|

A male child with 3 beta-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase deficiency cannot have which among the following?|Hypoglycemia|Normal genitalia|Increased DHEA|Decreased androstenedione|Normal genitalia|3 beta-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase deficiency causes ambiguous genitalia in males due to impaired androgen production.|Medium|Clinical|

A 10-month-old female baby, previously normal, suddenly becomes distressed in the crib. External genitalia is normal, except some hyperpigmentation. Blood glucose is 30 mg%. Most likely diagnosis?|CAH due to 21 hydroxylase deficiency|Hyperinsulinism|Familial glucocorticoid deficiency|Cushing syndrome|Familial glucocorticoid deficiency|Familial glucocorticoid deficiency presents with adrenal crisis, hypoglycemia, and hyperpigmentation due to ACTH excess.|Medium|Clinical|

Inheritance in congenital adrenal hyperplasia is?|Sporadic|Mitochondrial|Autosomal dominant|Autosomal recessive|Autosomal recessive|All forms of CAH, including 21-hydroxylase deficiency, are inherited in an autosomal recessive pattern.|Easy|General|

Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology
Chapter-Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH)
Topic-CAH Variants
A 16-year-old female presents with primary amenorrhea, absent axillary and pubic hair, and a short vagina. The diagnosis of Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome (AIS) is made. Which of the following is also a cause of hypergonadotropic hypogonadism?|Hypothyroidism|Klinefelter syndrome|Malnutrition|Cystic fibrosis

Klinefelter syndrome



Hypergonadotropic hypogonadism is caused by primary gonadal failure, leading to elevated FSH and LH. Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome (AIS): 46,XY karyotype with female external genitalia, no uterus, cryptorchidism. Testicular feminization due to defective androgen receptor → body cannot respond to testosterone. Testosterone is converted to estrogen → feminization (breast development). Management: Gonadectomy (to prevent malignancy) + estrogen therapy. Other causes of hypergonadotropic hypogonadism: Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY) → Small testes, gynecomastia, infertility Turner syndrome (45,X) → Streak ovaries, primary amenorrhea|Medium|Clinical|

A 5-year-old boy with precocious puberty and well-developed pubic hair with normal genitalia and BP 120/90. Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology
Chapter-Addison Disease
Topic-Addison Disease
An 8-year-old boy in the post-operative ward develops increased thirst and urination 6 hours after he underwent neurosurgery to remove craniopharyngioma. His blood pressure is 100/60mmHg, and his random blood sugar level is 58mg/dl. The clinician identifies it as a case of panhypopituitarism following neurosurgery. Which of the following is the next best step in managing this patient?|Hydrocortisone|Thyroxine|Growth hormone|Prolactin

Hydrocortisone



Panhypopituitarism results in secondary adrenal insufficiency due to a lack of ACTH, leading to hypotension and hypoglycemia. Hydrocortisone (glucocorticoid replacement) must be given first before initiating thyroxine, as untreated adrenal insufficiency may cause an adrenal crisis. Other hormone replacements (thyroxine, GH) are considered after stabilization.|Medium|Clinical|

A 7-year-old female presents with a neck mass, cold intolerance, constipation, and a mass that moves with swallowing. What is the least likely association with this condition?|Growth retardation|Mental retardation|Delayed puberty|Pseudohypertrophy of muscles

Pseudohypertrophy of muscles



Likely diagnosis: Hypothyroidism (goiter). Common associations: Growth retardation, mental retardation (if untreated) Delayed puberty Pseudohypertrophy (e.g., in Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy) is not a feature of hypothyroidism.|Medium|Clinical|

A 2-year-old child presents with short stature, constipation, lethargy, delayed dentition, and a firm nodular goiter. What is the appropriate treatment?|4-6 mg/kg/day of Thyroxine|2-4 mg/kg/day of Thyroxine|6-8 mg/kg/day of Thyroxine|8-10 mg/kg/day of Thyroxine

4-6 mg/kg/day of Thyroxine



Congenital or acquired hypothyroidism can lead to growth retardation, constipation, and goiter. Treatment dose: Neonates: 10-15 mcg/kg/day Infants (1-12 months): 8-10 mcg/kg/day Toddlers (1-3 years): 4-6 mcg/kg/day Importance: Early treatment prevents intellectual disability and growth failure.|Easy|Clinical|

An 18-year-old female with anorexia nervosa has hypogonadotropic hypogonadism. Which of the following is least likely to be associated with this condition?|Both LH and FSH are normal|Both LH and FSH are decreased|Chronic systemic illness is a reversible cause|Trauma to the pituitary gland leads to permanent hypogonadotropic hypogonadism

Both LH and FSH are normal



Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism is characterized by low LH and FSH due to a problem in the hypothalamic-pituitary axis. Common causes: Anorexia nervosa (functional suppression due to low energy state) Chronic illness (e.g., Celiac disease, CKD) CNS disorders (hypothalamic tumors, pituitary trauma, Kallmann syndrome) Permanent hypogonadotropic hypogonadism occurs in trauma-related pituitary damage.|Medium|General|

An 8-day-old neonate presents with vomiting, dehydration, hyperpigmentation, and ambiguous genitalia. Laboratory findings show hypoglycemia, hyponatremia, and hyperkalemia. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Pyloric stenosis|Hypothyroidism|Hyperaldosteronism|Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH)

Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH)



CAH (21-hydroxylase deficiency) leads to: Cortisol deficiency → Hypoglycemia, vomiting Aldosterone deficiency → Hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, dehydration Excess androgens → Ambiguous genitalia in females Diagnosis: Elevated 17-hydroxyprogesterone, ACTH stimulation test. Treatment: Hydrocortisone (glucocorticoid) + Fludrocortisone (mineralocorticoid)|Medium|Clinical|

Most common cause of Addison disease in children is?|Idiopathic|Autoimmune destruction|Infectious causes|Tubercular|Autoimmune destruction|Autoimmune adrenalitis is the leading cause of Addison disease in children, characterized by destruction of the adrenal cortex.|Medium|General|

Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology
Chapter-Bone Age and Growth Disorders
Topic-Advanced Bone Age
A 12-year-old male presents to the pediatric outpatient department due to short stature. Physical examination reveals mild obesity, round face, and a micropenis. Height is below normal (>2 standard deviations low). The clinician identified it as a case of growth hormone deficiency and started GH replacement. Which of the following shows the effect of growth hormone on the upper segment: lower segment ratio (US: LS) for this patient?|Increase in the US: LS|Decrease in the US: LS|No change in the US: LS|No change followed by an increase in the US: LS

Increase in the US: LS



Growth hormone deficiency (GHD) leads to proportionate short stature and increased upper segment to lower segment ratio (US: LS). GH replacement increases linear growth, but initially, the torso grows faster than the limbs, further increasing US: LS ratio before normalizing.|Medium|Clinical|

A 4-week-old female infant presents with poor feeding, prolonged jaundice, lethargy, large anterior fontanelle, macroglossia, and umbilical hernia. Which of the following is NOT associated with this condition?|It is the most common cause of intellectual disability.|Thyroxine is useful in treatment.|Thyroid dysgenesis is the most common cause.|Screening should be done after 1-2 days of birth.

Screening should be done after 1-2 days of birth.



Condition: Congenital hypothyroidism Screening: Should be done after 48 hours of birth, not immediately, as maternal T4 still affects the neonate. Key points: Most common cause of intellectual disability (if untreated). Thyroid dysgenesis (absent or ectopic thyroid) is the most common cause. Treatment: Levothyroxine immediately.|Medium|Clinical|

A 4-month-old baby presents with a hoarse cry, protruding tongue, wide open anterior and posterior fontanelles, hypotonia, hypothermia, abdominal distension, and umbilical hernia. Blood tests show low T4 and TSH 200mU/L. What is the diagnosis?|Congenital hypothyroidism|Down syndrome|Acquired hypothyroidism|Rett syndrome

Congenital hypothyroidism



Congenital hypothyroidism is a leading cause of preventable intellectual disability. Classic features: Hypotonia, hoarse cry, large tongue, umbilical hernia Wide-open fontanelles (delayed closure) Low T4, markedly high TSH (>100 mU/L) Diagnosis: Newborn screening (TSH and T4 levels). Treatment: Levothyroxine (10-15 mcg/kg/day) immediately to prevent intellectual disability.|Medium|Clinical|

A 7-year-old female presents with bilateral breast tissue formation and pubic hair. The clinician diagnoses central precocious puberty. What is the most common cause of central precocious puberty?|Exogenous estrogen|Idiopathic|CNS tumor|Hypothyroidism

Idiopathic



Central Precocious Puberty (CPP) is gonadotropin-dependent puberty due to early activation of the hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal (HPG) axis. Most common cause: Idiopathic (in 90% of girls). Other causes: CNS lesions (e.g., hypothalamic hamartomas, gliomas, astrocytomas) Radiation, trauma, infections (meningitis, encephalitis) Diagnosis: Elevated LH/FSH, advanced bone age. Treatment: GnRH analogs (e.g., leuprolide).|Medium|Clinical|

A 16-year-old girl is brought to the clinic due to absence of menarche. The doctor orders tests for LH and FSH levels, which show low values. Which of the following is NOT an etiology of this condition?|Severe malnutrition|Prader-Willi Syndrome|CNS malformation|Hypothyroidism

Hypothyroidism



Delayed puberty can be classified into hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (low LH/FSH) or hypergonadotropic hypogonadism (high LH/FSH). Causes of hypogonadotropic hypogonadism: Severe malnutrition (e.g., anorexia nervosa) Chronic illness (e.g., CKD, cystic fibrosis, celiac disease) CNS malformations (e.g., congenital hypopituitarism, Kallmann syndrome) Prader-Willi syndrome (hypotonia, obesity, short stature, mental retardation, hypogonadism) Hypothyroidism is NOT a primary cause of hypogonadotropic hypogonadism. Severe hypothyroidism can lead to delayed puberty, but it primarily affects metabolism, not direct gonadal suppression.|Medium|Clinical|

Bone age advanced of chronological age is seen in?|GH deficiency|Precocious puberty|Hypothyroidism|Constitutional delay in growth|Precocious puberty|Precocious puberty leads to advanced bone maturation due to increased sex steroids, while other conditions delay bone age.|Medium|General|

Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology

Chapter-Genetic Syndromes
Topic-Soto's Syndrome

What is the most likely diagnosis?|Carpenter syndrome|Alstrom syndrome|Laurence-Moon-Bardet-Biedl syndrome|Prader-Willi syndrome

Laurence-Moon-Bardet-Biedl syndrome



Laurence-Moon-Bardet-Biedl syndrome is an autosomal recessive disorder characterized by: Retinal degeneration (pigmentary retinopathy leading to vision loss). Obesity and polydactyly. Developmental delay and hypogonadism. Diagnosis: Genetic testing for BBS gene mutations. Treatment: Supportive care for vision loss, weight management, and hormonal therapy.|Tough|Clinical|

Prader-Willi Syndrome



Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) is a genetic disorder caused by paternal deletion of chromosome 15q11-13. Features: Hypotonia in infancy Hyperphagia → Obesity Short stature, hypogonadism, cognitive impairment Diagnosis: Methylation analysis for genetic confirmation.|Medium|Clinical|

A baby is examined at birth, and further investigations reveal: External genitalia: Female Absent uterus Blind-ending vagina Intra-abdominal testes Which of the following is NOT true regarding this condition?|Previously called 'Testicular Feminisation Syndrome'|Basic defect – Resistance to androgens|It is the most common form of 46, XY Differences in Sex Development|Autosomal dominant inheritance

Autosomal dominant inheritance



The condition described is Androgen Insensitivity Syndrome (AIS), previously called Testicular Feminization Syndrome. Cause: Mutations in the Androgen Receptor (AR) gene, leading to resistance to testosterone. Features: 46, XY karyotype but female phenotype Absent uterus, cryptorchidism, infertility Normal breast development but absent pubic/axillary hair Inheritance: X-linked recessive (not autosomal dominant).|Tough|Clinical|

A 16-year-old male presents with delayed puberty and anosmia. Lab reports show low LH, FSH, and testosterone levels. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Klinefelter syndrome|Kallmann syndrome|Androgen insensitivity syndrome|Prader-Willi syndrome

Kallmann syndrome



Kallmann Syndrome is a form of congenital hypogonadotropic hypogonadism due to a defect in migration of GnRH neurons and associated with anosmia (absent sense of smell). Key features: Hypogonadism (delayed puberty, infertility, micropenis, cryptorchidism) Anosmia or hyposmia Associated with midline defects (cleft lip/palate, renal agenesis) Diagnosis: Low LH/FSH/testosterone, MRI showing absent olfactory bulbs. Treatment: Testosterone replacement or GnRH therapy.|Medium|Clinical|

A 7-year-old girl presents to the pediatric clinic with early-onset puberty. On examination, she has Tanner stage 3 breast development. The doctor prescribes Leuprolide. Which of the following statements is incorrect regarding this condition?|LH is a better indicator of puberty than FSH|LH levels > 0.6 mU/L or LH/FSH ratio >1 indicates development of puberty|Advanced bone age|CT scan is the best imaging modality to rule out CNS, gonadal, and adrenal tumors

CT scan is the best imaging modality to rule out CNS, gonadal, and adrenal tumors



Precocious puberty is defined as the appearance of secondary sexual characteristics before age 8 in girls and 9 in boys. Leuprolide (GnRH agonist) is used to suppress central precocious puberty (CPP) by downregulating GnRH receptors. Key diagnostic markers: LH > 0.6 mU/L or LH/FSH ratio >1 → Suggests true puberty activation Advanced bone age (due to premature estrogen exposure) Correct imaging modality: MRI brain is preferred to rule out CNS tumors (e.g., hypothalamic hamartomas, gliomas, craniopharyngiomas, germinomas). CT scan is NOT the best initial modality.|Medium|Clinical|

Not seen in Sotos syndrome?|Macrocephaly|Hypertelorism|Mongoloid slant of eyes|Ataxic gait|Mongoloid slant of eyes|Sotos syndrome is associated with macrocephaly, hypertelorism, and overgrowth, but not mongoloid slant of eyes.|Medium|General|

Unit-Pediatric Endocrinology
Chapter-Diabetes Mellitus
Topic-HbA1c
An 8-year-old girl is brought to the emergency department due to an episode of unconsciousness for 5 minutes. She has a history of type 1 diabetes mellitus for 2 years and is taking insulin irregularly. Her HbA1c level is 9%. What is the target level of HbA1c for this patient?|< 8%|< 7.5%|< 7%|< 6.5%

< 7.5%



Target HbA1c levels for children with Type 1 Diabetes: <7.5% (per ADA guidelines). Poor glycemic control (HbA1c >9%) increases the risk of diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) and microvascular complications. Management: Insulin therapy optimization, dietary adjustments, and regular monitoring.|Medium|Clinical|

An 8-year-old girl with Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus presents with unconsciousness for 5 minutes and HbA1c of 9%. What is the target level of HbA1c for this patient?|< 8%|< 7.5%|< 7%|< 6.5%

< 7.5%



Target HbA1c levels for children with Type 1 DM: <7.5% (per ADA guidelines). Poor glycemic control (HbA1c >9%) increases risk of diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) and microvascular complications. Management: Insulin therapy optimization, dietary adjustments, and regular monitoring.|Medium|Clinical|

A 7-year-old male with obesity, polydactyly, and retinitis pigmentosa is diagnosed with Laurence-Moon-Biedl syndrome. Which of the following is the least likely association?|Hypogonadism|Mental retardation|Retinitis pigmentosa|Cataract

Cataract



Laurence-Moon-Biedl Syndrome (LMBS) is a rare autosomal recessive disorder. Key features: Obesity, hypogonadism, polydactyly, mental retardation Retinitis pigmentosa (progressive vision loss) Cataracts are NOT associated with this syndrome.|Easy|Clinical|

Diabetes is diagnosed if any of the following are present: Fasting plasma glucose ≥126 mg/dL Random plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL + symptoms HbA1c ≥6.5% 2-hour post-glucose challenge plasma glucose ≥200 mg/dL Plasma glucose >124 mg/dL after glucose ingestion is NOT diagnostic.|Easy|General|

Cushing Syndrome (Hypercortisolism): Caused by prolonged exposure to high cortisol levels Features: Obesity, moon facies, buffalo hump, purple striae, muscle weakness, hypertension Treatment: Ketoconazole (inhibits adrenal steroid synthesis) Mitotane or metyrapone for severe cases Transsphenoidal surgery if pituitary adenoma is present|Medium|Clinical|

Which of the following is NOT a clinical parameter used to diagnose obesity?|BMI|Skin fold thickness|Waist circumference and waist-hip ratio|Mid-upper arm circumference

A 5-year-old boy is brought to the physician because of an increased appetite. He has several episodes of binge eating and gets angry when his parents refuse to offer him additional food. The patient has a history of bilateral cryptorchidism and hypotonia. He also suffers from developmental delays and takes regular speech and physical therapies. On examination, he has a thin upper lip, almond-shaped eyes, downturned mouth corners, and slight strabismus. Which of the following is the most likely etiology of this patient's condition?|Microdeletion of the maternal copy of chromosome 15|Microdeletion of the paternal copy of chromosome 15|Robertsonian translocation of chromosome 21|Mosaicism|Microdeletion of the paternal copy of chromosome 15|Lesch-Nyhan Syndrome is caused by the deficiency of HGPRT due to mutations in the HPRT1 gene, leading to hyperuricemia, self-mutilation, and neurodevelopmental delays.|Tough|Clinical|

A 3-year-old baby is brought to the physician by his parents to be evaluated for increased fatigue. His parents noticed that he was becoming weaker and had difficulty walking. He has also been struggling to climb stairs for the past few months. The patient cannot stand up from the floor without the help of his hands. He is vitally stable and is currently not taking any medications. On examination, his calf muscles are enlarged, and his pelvic muscles appear weak. Karyotype analysis reflected nondisjunction of chromosome 21, while karyotypes of mother and father are normal. Which of the following is the most significant feature of nondisjunction?|Euploidy|Normal separation of chromosomes during cell division|It occurs in meiosis only|Mosaicism|Mosaicism|Mosaicism results from nondisjunction after fertilization, leading to different cell populations with varying chromosome numbers.|Medium|Clinical|

Which disorders are associated with malignancy?|Down's syndrome, Fragile X syndrome, Bloom syndrome, Fanconi's anemia|Fragile X-syndrome, Bloom syndrome, Fanconi's anemia, Kostmann syndrome|Down's syndrome, Bloom syndrome, Fanconi's anemia, Kostmann syndrome|Fragile X-syndrome, Bloom syndrome, Fanconi's anemia|Down's syndrome, Bloom syndrome, Fanconi's anemia, Kostmann syndrome|Down syndrome, Fanconi anemia, and Bloom syndrome are linked to increased malignancy risk, particularly leukemia.|Medium|General|

At room temperature, it can be stored for up to 6 hours, and in the refrigerator, it remains safe for up to 24 hours.|Medium|General|

Unit-Pediatric Immunology
Chapter-B-Cell and T-Cell Disorders
Topic-Immunodeficiency Disorders
A 5-month-old boy is brought to the physician because of a fever and a cough for 3 days and multiple episodes of loose stools over the past 3 months. He has been treated for otitis media four times and bronchiolitis three times during the past three months. He was born at 37 weeks gestation, and the neonatal period was uncomplicated. He is at the 10th percentile for height and the 3rd percentile for weight. Examination shows an erythematous, scaly rash over the trunk and extremities. White patches on the tongue and buccal mucosa bleed when scraped. Which of the following is most related to the condition of this child?|It is due to adenosine deaminase deficiency|Humoral immunity is predominantly affected|Child should receive a double dose of all vaccines|Patients present with an enlarged thymus

It is due to adenosine deaminase deficiency

Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID) is caused by a defect in adenosine deaminase, leading to T-cell dysfunction, recurrent infections, chronic diarrhea, and failure to thrive. Oral thrush, otitis media, and bronchiolitis are common.

Tough|Clinical|

A 2-year-old boy is brought to the physician for the evaluation of fever, difficulty breathing, and cough for the past week. In the past, he has been treated for sinus infections and bronchiolitis. Since birth, he has had multiple episodes of oral thrush treated with nystatin, chronic diarrhea, and failure to thrive. His vitals are in the normal range. He has a prominent nasal bridge, a shortened chin, and dysplastic ears on examination. He has a deficiency of T lymphocytes. Which of the following diseases is present with T-cell defects?|Severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID)|Common variable immunodeficiency|Wiskott-Aldrich syndrome|DiGeorge syndrome

DiGeorge syndrome

DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion) is characterized by T-cell deficiency due to thymic aplasia, leading to recurrent infections, facial dysmorphism, congenital heart defects, and hypocalcemia.

Medium|Clinical|

A 4-year-old boy with complement C8 deficiency is susceptible to which of the following conditions?|SLE|Hereditary angioedema|Pneumococcal infections|Meningococcal infections

Meningococcal infections

Deficiency of terminal complement components (C5-C9) leads to increased susceptibility to Neisseria meningitidis infections due to impaired formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC).

Medium|Clinical|

A 4-year-old boy is brought to a pediatrician due to recurrent skin lesions. The lesions first appeared two months ago and have not responded to any prescribed treatment. His medical history is significant for recurrent respiratory tract infections. His older brother has bronchial asthma. He is at the 5th percentile for length and the 10th percentile for weight. Examination shows several raised, erythematous lesions over the face, neck, groin, and extremities; some secrete pus. Cervical and axillary lymph nodes are enlarged bilaterally. What is the most likely diagnosis?|X-linked agammaglobulinemia|Ataxia-telangiectasia|Atopic dermatitis|Chronic granulomatous disease

Chronic granulomatous disease

Chronic granulomatous disease (CGD) is due to NADPH oxidase deficiency, leading to defective phagocytic killing. Patients present with recurrent infections, lymphadenopathy, and granuloma formation.

Tough|Clinical|

A 2-year-old boy is brought to a pediatric hospital by his mother for evaluation of recurrent infections and easy bruising. He has been hospitalized three times for severe skin and respiratory infections, responding to antibiotic treatment. Examination shows sparse, silvery hair. The skin is hypopigmented, and there are diffuse petechiae. Laboratory studies show pancytopenia. A peripheral blood smear shows giant cytoplasmic granules in granulocytes and platelets. What is the most likely underlying cause of this patient's symptoms?|Defective NADPH oxidase|The defective IL-2R gamma chain|Defective tyrosine kinase gene|Defective lysosomal trafficking regulator gene

Defective lysosomal trafficking regulator gene

Chediak-Higashi syndrome is caused by mutations in the LYST gene, leading to giant cytoplasmic granules in neutrophils, recurrent infections, oculocutaneous albinism, and neuropathy.

Tough|Clinical|

Not a B-cell defect?|CVID|Chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis|Hyper IgM syndrome|X-linked agammaglobulinemia|Chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis|Chronic mucocutaneous candidiasis is caused by a T-cell defect, not a B-cell defect. The other options are related to B-cell abnormalities.|Medium|General|

Unit-Pediatric Immunology
Chapter-B-Cell and T-Cell Disorders
Topic-SCID Variants
A 10-month-old boy is brought to the physician because of fever and a cough for three days. He was treated for otitis media previously. He was also treated for pneumococcal pneumonia and H. influenzae meningitis last month. The pregnancy and neonatal period were uncomplicated. He was exclusively breastfed until six months of age. He has a maternal uncle who died of recurrent respiratory tract infections at age 28. He is at the 20th percentile for height and the 10th percentile for weight. His temperature is 39°C (102.2°F), his pulse is 122/min, and his respirations are 44/min. Examination shows crackles at the left lung base. Palatine tonsils are absent. An X-ray shows an infiltrate in the left lower lung lobe. Flow cytometry shows the absence of B cells. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patient's late onset of symptoms?|Involution of the thymus begins at six months|Maternal IgG decreases by six months|Splenic dysfunction occurs in 6 months|Breastfeeding until six months

Maternal IgG decreases by six months

X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton’s disease) is characterized by a lack of B cells and recurrent bacterial infections, which become evident after maternal IgG wanes (around 6 months).

Tough|Clinical|

What is the diagnosis for a 7-month-old boy presenting with fever, irritability, purulent ear discharge, hypoplastic adenoids without exudate, markedly low levels of B cells, and high levels of T cells on flow cytometry?|DiGeorge syndrome|Common variable immunodeficiency|Transient hypogammaglobulinemia of infancy|X-linked agammaglobulinemia

X-linked agammaglobulinemia

X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's disease) is due to a BTK gene mutation, leading to absence of B cells, hypoplastic lymphoid tissue, and recurrent bacterial infections.

Medium|Clinical|

Unit-Pediatric Immunology
Chapter-Phagocytic Disorders
Topic-Diagnostic Tools
A 2-year-old boy is brought to the physician by his mother for recurrent infections and bruising. Chediak-Higashi syndrome is a rare autosomal recessive disorder caused by defective lysosomal trafficking regulator gene (LYST), leading to abnormal lysosomal fusion, recurrent infections, hypopigmentation, neuropathy, and bleeding tendency.

Unit-Pediatric Immunology
Chapter-Phagocytic Disorders
Topic-GI Involvement

Unit-Pediatric Immunology
Chapter-Complement Defects
Topic-Clinical Manifestations
A 4-month-old male baby presents to the pediatrician with persistent diarrhea. The patient had a history of recurrent attacks of pneumonia and otitis media, for which he had to be hospitalized. No live vaccines were administered to the patient. Further history reveals the diagnosis of severe combined immunodeficiency. Which of the following features is associated with the condition of this patient?|Autoimmune diseases|Granuloma formation|GVHD|Graft rejection

GVHD

SCID patients lack both B and T cell function, making them highly susceptible to graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) if they receive non-irradiated blood products.

Metachromatic leukodystrophy (MLD) is a lysosomal storage disorder due to arylsulfatase A deficiency, causing progressive neurodegeneration, optic atrophy, and exaggerated reflexes but not decerebrate posturing.

Unit-Pediatric Immunology

What is the cause of the symptoms in a 4-month-old girl presenting with recurrent infections, delayed umbilical cord separation, white mucosal patches with bleeding upon scraping, nonpurulent ear discharge, scaly erythematous skin lesions on the chest, and a leukocyte count of 38,700/mm³ with 90% neutrophils?|Chronic granulomatous disease|Chediak Higashi syndrome|Leukocyte adhesion deficiency type 1 (LAD1)|Myeloperoxidase deficiency

Unit-Pediatric Immunology
Chapter-Genetic Immunodeficiency
Topic-FOXP3 Mutation
A 5-year-old boy is brought to the physician because of a sore throat and pain for 3 days. He had 4-5 episodes of oral thrush last year and has been treated for it. He also has fungal patches on his head. Examination shows white patches on the tongue and palate. When scraping off the patches, there is erythematous oral mucosa with slight bleeding. Which of the following is a feature of the patient’s presenting condition?|Defective production of prostaglandins|Defective rolling of neutrophils|Ability to produce hydroxyl halide radicals|Defective functioning of the MPO-H₂O₂ system

Ability to produce hydroxyl halide radicals

Nitroblue tetrazolium (NBT) test turning dark blue indicates normal production of reactive oxygen species (ROS), ruling out chronic granulomatous disease (CGD).

Hyper IgE syndrome (Job syndrome) is a primary immunodeficiency disorder caused by STAT3 mutations, leading to recurrent staphylococcal infections, eczema, and high serum IgE levels.

Medium|General|

What is the diagnosis for a 7-month-old boy presenting with fever, irritability, purulent ear discharge, hypoplastic adenoids without exudate, markedly low levels of B cells, and high levels of T cells on flow cytometry?|DiGeorge syndrome|Common variable immunodeficiency|Transient hypogammaglobulinemia of infancy|X-linked agammaglobulinemia

X-linked agammaglobulinemia

X-linked agammaglobulinemia (Bruton's disease) is due to a BTK gene mutation, leading to absence of B cells, hypoplastic lymphoid tissue, and recurrent bacterial infections.

Unit-Pediatric Immunology
Chapter-Syndromic Immunodeficiencies
Topic-Ataxia Telangiectasia
A child was brought to the clinic by his mother due to unstable gait. On examination, oculocutaneous telangiectasias were present. Upon investigation, the child had low IgA, IgE, and IgG2 levels. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Ataxia Telangiectasia|Chronic Granulomatous Disease|DiGeorge Syndrome|Chediak-Higashi Syndrome

Ataxia Telangiectasia

Ataxia-telangiectasia is a rare autosomal recessive disorder caused by ATM gene mutations, leading to progressive neurodegeneration, telangiectasias, and immunodeficiency (low IgA, IgG, IgE).

Medium|Clinical|

A child was brought to the clinic by his mother due to unstable gait. On examination, oculocutaneous telangiectasias were present. Upon investigation, the child had low IgA, IgE, and IgG2 levels. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Ataxia Telangiectasia|Chronic Granulomatous Disease|DiGeorge Syndrome|Chediak Higashi Syndrome

Ataxia Telangiectasia

Ataxia-telangiectasia is caused by ATM gene mutations, leading to progressive cerebellar ataxia, telangiectasias, and immunodeficiency (low IgA, IgG, IgE). Patients are at risk of developing lymphoma and leukemia.

Medium|Clinical|

A group of students was studying primary immunodeficiency disorders. They discussed a disorder that presents with increased sinopulmonary infections. It is due to impaired differentiation of naive B cells into IgA-producing plasma cells. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Selective IgA Deficiency|Bruton's Disease|Hyper IgE Syndrome|Severe Combined Immunodeficiency

Selective IgA Deficiency

Selective IgA deficiency is the most common primary immunodeficiency, leading to recurrent sinopulmonary and gastrointestinal infections due to defective IgA production.

Unit-Pediatric Neurology
Chapter-Pediatric Epilepsy
Topic-Infantile Spasms
What is the most characteristic EEG pattern seen in infantile spasms?|Generalized sharp waves|Hypsarrhythmia|Triphasic waves|14 and 6 Hz positive spikes

Hypsarrhythmia|Infantile spasms (West syndrome) present with chaotic, high-amplitude, disorganized EEG (hypsarrhythmia). ACTH or vigabatrin is first-line therapy.|High|Clinical|

A 5-month-old male infant presents with sudden, repetitive jerking movements of his arms and legs. Examination reveals ash-leaf spots and periungual fibromas. What is the drug of choice for infantile spasms in this child?|Diazepam|ACTH|Levetiracetam|Vigabatrin

Vigabatrin



Infantile spasms (West syndrome) is characterized by sudden flexion, extension, or mixed flexion-extension movements of the neck, trunk, and limbs that last a few seconds and occur in clusters. This condition is commonly associated with tuberous sclerosis complex (TSC), as evident in this case due to ash-leaf spots and periungual fibromas. EEG in Infantile Spasms shows Hypsarrhythmia, a chaotic, high-voltage pattern of disorganized background activity with multifocal spikes. The first-line treatment for infantile spasms associated with TSC is Vigabatrin (GABA transaminase inhibitor) as it directly affects the underlying tuberous sclerosis pathology. ACTH is the drug of choice for idiopathic cases of infantile spasms, but in TSC-related spasms, Vigabatrin is preferred due to better efficacy and fewer side effects.|Tough|Clinical|

Drug of choice in infantile spasm?|ACTH|Vigabatrin|Carbamazepine|Ethosuximide|ACTH|ACTH (Adrenocorticotropic Hormone) is the first-line treatment for infantile spasms, especially in idiopathic cases.|Medium|Clinical|

Drug of choice in infantile spasm in tuberous sclerosis?|ACTH|Vigabatrin|Carbamazepine|Ethosuximide|Vigabatrin|Vigabatrin is the preferred treatment for infantile spasms associated with tuberous sclerosis due to its efficacy in such cases.|Medium|Clinical|

Topic-Partial Seizures
A 10-year-old boy presents with focal seizures characterized by clonic movements of the right hand, preserved consciousness, and postictal confusion. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Benign epilepsy with centrotemporal spikes (BECTS)|Lennox-Gastaut syndrome|Landau-Kleffner syndrome|West syndrome

Benign epilepsy with centrotemporal spikes (BECTS)|BECTS (Rolandic epilepsy) is the most common childhood focal epilepsy, presenting with focal seizures involving the face or upper limb, normal cognition, and centrotemporal spikes on EEG.|Medium|Clinical|

A 5-year-old child presents with two episodes of focal seizures with arm twitching and foamy mouth in 24 hours. The doctor orders an EEG. What is the distinguishing feature of this condition?|Generalized tonic-clonic seizures|Infantile spasms|Visual seizures|Focal seizures

Focal seizures



Focal (partial) seizures originate from a specific area in one hemisphere and may spread. This child exhibits focal motor seizures (arm twitching) with secondary generalization (foaming at the mouth). Focal seizures may or may not impair awareness. If awareness is preserved, it is focal aware seizure (simple partial seizure); if awareness is impaired, it is focal impaired awareness seizure (complex partial seizure). EEG in focal seizures shows focal epileptiform discharges in the affected lobe. The most common cause in children is structural brain abnormalities (e.g., cortical dysplasia, tuberous sclerosis, or previous perinatal insults like hypoxic-ischemic injury).|Medium|Clinical|

Drug of choice in simple partial seizure in children is?|Phenytoin|Valproic acid|Carbamazepine|Phenobarbitone|Carbamazepine|Carbamazepine is the first-line drug for treating simple partial seizures due to its safety and effectiveness.|Medium|General|

Automatisms are seen in?|Complex partial seizure|Simple partial seizures|Infantile spasms|Absence seizures|Complex partial seizure|Automatisms, such as lip-smacking or repetitive movements, are characteristic of complex partial seizures.|Medium|General|

A 4-year-old child presents with generalized tonic-clonic seizure associated with fever for 3 months. Neuroimaging reveals multiple ring-enhancing lesions with perifocal edema, and CBC shows eosinophilia. What is the drug of choice?|Mebendazole|Albendazole|Thiabendazole|Ivermectin

Albendazole



Neurocysticercosis (NCC) is the most common parasitic CNS infection worldwide, caused by Taenia solium (pork tapeworm). CT/MRI findings include multiple ring-enhancing lesions with surrounding edema, often in the gray-white junction of the brain. CSF may show eosinophilia and increased protein. Albendazole is the first-line treatment along with steroids (to reduce inflammation caused by dying cysts) and antiepileptic drugs (e.g., Levetiracetam, Valproate) for seizure control. Mebendazole is used for intestinal worm infections but not for neurocysticercosis.|Tough|Clinical|

A 3-year-old child with acute fever of 104°F developed febrile seizures. Tuberculous meningitis



Tuberculous meningitis (TBM) is a chronic meningeal infection due to Mycobacterium tuberculosis. It classically presents with slowly progressive fever, headache, vomiting, and altered sensorium, with neurological deficits in later stages. CSF findings in TBM: Lymphocytic predominance (100–500 cells/mm³) Elevated protein (>100 mg/dL) Low glucose (<45 mg/dL) Cobweb coagulum formation MRI shows basilar meningeal enhancement with hydrocephalus. First-line treatment: Anti-TB drugs (HRZE regimen) and steroids to reduce inflammation.|Tough|Clinical|

A 24-year-old male with a history of mental retardation and spastic limbs presents with worsening hip pain for 6 weeks. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Cerebral Palsy|Spinal Muscular Atrophy|Down Syndrome|Edward Syndrome

Cerebral Palsy



Cerebral palsy (CP) is a non-progressive motor disorder due to perinatal brain injury, causing spasticity, dystonia, and motor delay. Hip subluxation and dislocation are common in CP due to spasticity affecting hip adductors. Risk factors for CP: Preterm birth, perinatal asphyxia, intracranial hemorrhage Diagnosis: MRI shows periventricular leukomalacia (PVL). Management: Physical therapy Botulinum toxin injections Orthopedic surgery for contractures|Medium|Clinical|

Cryptococcus neoformans is an encapsulated yeast that causes fungal meningitis, primarily in immunocompromised patients (HIV, transplant recipients, steroid users). Spastic Diplegia



Spastic diplegia (a form of cerebral palsy) is characterized by lower limb stiffness, scissoring gait, and toe-walking due to hypertonia. MRI shows periventricular leukomalacia (PVL), due to perinatal ischemia affecting corticospinal tract fibers. Management includes: Physical therapy Botulinum toxin injections Selective dorsal rhizotomy (SDR) in severe cases.|Medium|Clinical|

A 20-day-old neonate presents with hypotonia, weak arms, uncontrolled movements, muscle spasms, and tremors. The diagnosis of bilirubin encephalopathy is confirmed. What type of palsy is commonly observed as a consequence of bilirubin encephalopathy in this patient?|Spastic Quadriplegia|Spastic Hemiplegia|Dyskinetic Cerebral Palsy|Hypotonic Cerebral Palsy

Dyskinetic Cerebral Palsy



Bilirubin encephalopathy (kernicterus) occurs due to excessive unconjugated bilirubin crossing the blood-brain barrier, damaging the basal ganglia, cerebellum, and brainstem nuclei. It leads to Dyskinetic (athetoid) cerebral palsy, characterized by: Choreoathetoid movements Hypotonia followed by rigidity Hearing loss (due to auditory nerve damage) Upward gaze palsy MRI in kernicterus: Bilateral hyperintensity in globus pallidus. Prevention: Phototherapy and exchange transfusion for neonatal hyperbilirubinemia.|Tough|Clinical|

What is the most likely diagnosis for this child's clinical presentation?|Trisomy 13|Spinal Muscular Atrophy|Poliomyelitis|Cerebral Palsy

Clinical Features: Floppy baby syndrome (severe hypotonia) Areflexia (absent deep tendon reflexes) Tongue fasciculations (a hallmark feature) Progressive weakness, leading to respiratory failure Diagnosis: Genetic testing for homozygous deletion of SMN1 EMG (Electromyography) showing denervation Management: Nusinersen (SMN2-targeted therapy) Gene therapy with Onasemnogene Abeparvovec Supportive care (ventilation, feeding support)|Tough|Clinical|

A 5-year-old boy with Duchenne muscular dystrophy (DMD) is started on an emerging therapy to slow disease progression. Which of the following drugs is most commonly used?|Prednisolone|Cyclophosphamide|Methotrexate|Cyclosporine

A 6-month-old male infant presents with hypotonia, areflexia, tongue fasciculations, and delayed motor milestones. What is the most likely diagnosis?|Trisomy 13|Spinal Muscular Atrophy|Poliomyelitis|Cerebral Palsy

Spinal Muscular Atrophy



Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA) is an autosomal recessive disorder due to mutation in the SMN1 gene, causing degeneration of anterior horn cells. Key features: Hypotonia (floppy baby syndrome) Areflexia (absent deep tendon reflexes) Tongue fasciculations Progressive weakness, especially proximal muscles Types of SMA: Type 1 (Werdnig-Hoffmann disease): Most severe, presents before 6 months, death by 2 years. Type 2: Intermediate severity, survives into childhood. Type 3 & 4: Less severe, presents later in life. Treatment: Gene therapy (Nusinersen, Risdiplam) and supportive care (physical therapy, ventilation support).|Tough|Clinical|

Drisapersen is a new drug effective in which among the following pediatric disorders?|Myasthenia gravis|Guillain-Barré syndrome|Friedrich ataxia|Duchenne’s muscular dystrophy|Duchenne’s muscular dystrophy|Drisapersen is an exon-skipping therapy that targets mutations in the dystrophin gene, effective in treating Duchenne’s muscular dystrophy.|Medium|General|
