Chapter-Vasculitis in Children
Topic-Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP)
What is the diagnosis for a 6-year-old boy presenting with abdominal pain, ankle joint pain, a non-blanching rash on his legs and buttocks, and findings of hematuria and proteinuria on urinalysis?|Buerger disease|Henoch-Schönlein purpura|Kawasaki disease|Systemic lupus erythematosus

Henoch-Schönlein purpura

HSP is a small-vessel vasculitis commonly affecting children, characterized by purpuric rash, arthritis, abdominal pain, and renal involvement.

Medium|Clinical|

The most common leukocytoclastic vasculitis affecting children is?|Takayasu disease|Mucocutaneous lymph node syndrome (Kawasaki disease)|Henoch-Schönlein Purpura|Polyarteritis nodosa|Henoch-Schönlein Purpura|Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP) is the most common small-vessel vasculitis in children, characterized by purpura, arthritis, abdominal pain, and renal involvement.|Medium|Clinical|

Henoch-Schönlein Purpura commonly involves which age group?|4–8 years|10–15 years|15–20 years|Above 20 years|4–8 years|HSP is most commonly seen in children aged 4–8 years and is rare in older children or adults.|Medium|General|

A 5-year-old child presents with perivascular IgA deposition and neutrophilic collection. There is erythematous rash on the lower limbs and non-blanching purpura. Probable diagnosis is?|Henoch-Schönlein Purpura|Wegener’s granulomatosis|Cryoglobulinemia|Kawasaki disease|Henoch-Schönlein Purpura|Perivascular IgA deposition and neutrophilic infiltration are hallmark findings in HSP, along with palpable purpura and systemic symptoms.|Medium|Clinical|

Which of the following is not seen in Henoch-Schönlein Purpura?|Purpura|Abdominal pain|Seizures|Arthralgia|Seizures|HSP primarily involves the skin, joints, gastrointestinal tract, and kidneys but does not typically present with seizures.|Easy|General|

Which immunoglobulin gets deposited perivascularly in HSP?|IgM|IgG|IgA|IgD|IgA|IgA deposition in small vessel walls is a characteristic feature of HSP.|Easy|General|

In HSP, renal involvement can occur up to what time after diagnosis?|Within 1 month|Within 2 months|Within 4 months|Within 6 months|Within 6 months|Renal manifestations such as hematuria or proteinuria in HSP can develop within 6 months after the initial presentation, requiring long-term follow-up.|Medium|General|

Which immunoglobulin gets deposited perivascularly in HSP?|IgM|IgG|IgA|IgD|IgA|IgA deposition in the small vessel walls is the hallmark finding in Henoch-Schönlein Purpura.|Easy|General|

Not seen in Henoch-Schönlein Purpura?|Purpura on buttocks|Renal involvement|Arthritis|Thrombocytopenia|Thrombocytopenia|Platelet counts are typically normal in HSP, and thrombocytopenia is not a characteristic feature.|Easy|General|

Unit-Paediatric Rheumatology and Vasculitis
Chapter-Kawasaki Disease
Topic-Diagnosis And Treatment
What is the diagnosis for a 3-year-old boy presenting with bilateral conjunctivitis, dry cracked lips with a strawberry tongue, palpable lymph node, grade II/VI systolic ejection murmur, and coronary artery aneurysm seen on angiography?|Kawasaki disease|Henoch-Schönlein purpura|Buerger disease|Systemic lupus erythematosus

Kawasaki disease

Kawasaki disease is a medium-vessel vasculitis affecting children, presenting with fever, mucocutaneous inflammation, and coronary artery aneurysms if untreated.

Medium|Clinical|

Select the most appropriate statement about a 4-year-old boy presenting with fever, a body rash, cervical lymphadenopathy, erythematous palms, and soles with peeling skin.|It is an acute febrile mucocutaneous lymph node syndrome|It is not a common vasculitic disorder in childhood|In the acute stage, only aspirin is used in the treatment|In the convalescent stage, both IVIG and aspirin are used in the treatment

It is an acute febrile mucocutaneous lymph node syndrome

Kawasaki disease is also called "acute febrile mucocutaneous lymph node syndrome," requiring IVIG and aspirin to prevent coronary artery complications.

Easy|General|

Not seen in Kawasaki disease?|Fever lasting more than 4 weeks|Desquamation after 2–3 weeks of disease onset|Raised acute phase reactants|High risk of cardiac complications|Fever lasting more than 4 weeks|Fever in Kawasaki disease typically lasts <4 weeks, and prolonged fever may indicate an alternative diagnosis.|Medium|General|

Familial form of sarcoidosis shows which type of inheritance?|Autosomal Recessive|Autosomal Dominant|X-linked Dominant|X-linked Recessive|Autosomal Dominant|Familial sarcoidosis is a rare form with autosomal dominant inheritance, characterized by granulomatous inflammation.|Easy|General|

Incidence of coronary artery aneurysms in untreated Kawasaki disease?|<5%|5–10%|15–25%|40–60%|15–25%|In untreated Kawasaki disease, 15–25% of children develop coronary artery aneurysms, making early diagnosis and treatment essential.|Medium|Clinical|

A 2-year-old child was diagnosed as having Kawasaki disease and was given IVIG in a dose of 2 g/kg. 36 hours later, the child continues to be febrile and has significant conjunctival and mucosal erythema. What next needs to be done?|Wait and watch|Repeat dose of IVIG|Start corticosteroids|Start Etanercept|Repeat dose of IVIG|If fever persists 36 hours after the first dose of IVIG, a second dose is recommended before considering corticosteroids or other immunosuppressants.|Medium|Clinical|

Indication of Prednisolone in Kawasaki disease?|Giant coronary aneurysms|Age <1 year|Massive thrombocytosis with ESR >50/hr|Failure to make patient afebrile despite 2 courses of IVIG|Failure to make patient afebrile despite 2 courses of IVIG|Corticosteroids are used in refractory Kawasaki disease cases unresponsive to 2 courses of IVIG.|Medium|Clinical|

All of the following are features to diagnose Kawasaki disease, except?|Myocarditis|High fever lasting at least 5 days|Desquamation and rash|Erythematous changes in the oral mucosa|Myocarditis|Kawasaki disease does not include myocarditis as a diagnostic criterion, although it may occur as a complication.|Medium|General|

Drug of choice in Kawasaki disease?|Prednisolone|IVIG|Rituximab|Infliximab|IVIG|IVIG is the first-line treatment for Kawasaki disease and significantly reduces the risk of coronary artery aneurysms.|Medium|Clinical|

Incidence of coronary artery aneurysms in untreated Kawasaki disease?|<5%|5–10%|15–25%|40–60%|15–25%|The risk of coronary artery aneurysms in untreated Kawasaki disease is 15–25%, making early treatment essential.|Medium|General|

Unit-Paediatric Rheumatology and Vasculitis
Chapter-Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis (JIA)
Topic-Subtypes and Features
Which congenital heart anomaly is likely to be present in the baby of a 30-year-old woman with a history of photosensitive malar rash, non-erosive arthritis, anemia of chronic disease, and thrombocytopenia?|Atrial septal defect|Tetralogy of Fallot|Transposition of great vessels|Complete heart block

Complete heart block

Neonatal lupus syndrome, associated with maternal SLE, can cause congenital complete heart block due to transplacental passage of anti-Ro/SSA antibodies.

Medium|Clinical|

Which laboratory study is most likely to confirm the diagnosis in a 12-year-old girl presenting with intermittent fever, arthralgias, weight loss, facial erythema, oral ulcers, palpable liver, microscopic hematuria, proteinuria, and decreased C3 and C4 levels?|Anti-double-stranded DNA antibody|Antinuclear antibody|Anti-Ro and anti-La antibody|Anti-histone antibody

Anti-double-stranded DNA antibody

SLE is confirmed by anti-dsDNA antibodies, which correlate with disease severity and nephritis.

Medium|Clinical|

A patient presents to you with complaints of loss of appetite for 4 months, weight loss, fever, chest discomfort, hemoptysis, and shortness of breath. Which of the following is characteristic of the diagnosis?|It is a form of cancer|Focal necrotizing glomerulonephritis|Positive for p-ANCA|Involves large vessels

Focal necrotizing glomerulonephritis

Granulomatosis with polyangiitis (Wegener’s) is a small-vessel vasculitis causing focal necrotizing glomerulonephritis, lung involvement, and sinusitis.

Tough|Clinical|

Select the correct diagnosis for a 13-year-old girl presenting with recurrent sinusitis, epistaxis, weight loss, blood-stained cough, hematuria, shortness of breath, hypertension, and a renal biopsy showing necrotizing granuloma, along with a positive anti-proteinase-3 ANCA.|Polyarteritis Nodosa|Wegener's granulomatosis|Microscopic polyangiitis|Churg-Strauss syndrome

Wegener's granulomatosis

Granulomatosis with polyangiitis (GPA/Wegener’s) is a necrotizing granulomatous vasculitis affecting the upper respiratory tract, lungs, and kidneys, with c-ANCA positivity.

Tough|Clinical|

A 10-year-old female child presents with oral and genital ulcers, periorbital pain, and aphthous stomatitis. Her family originates from Turkey. Which of the following is the most probable diagnosis?|Giant cell arteritis|Behcet's disease|Wegener's granulomatosis|Henoch-Schönlein purpura

Behcet's disease

Behcet's disease is a vasculitis causing oral and genital ulcers, uveitis, and thrombosis, more common in individuals of Middle Eastern and Mediterranean descent.

Medium|Clinical|

Which among the following presents with rash, symmetrical weakness of proximal muscles, elevated levels of CPK, SGOT, IDH, and necrosis on muscle biopsy?|Kawasaki disease|Juvenile Dermatomyositis|Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP)|Wegener's granulomatosis

Juvenile Dermatomyositis

Juvenile dermatomyositis (JDM) presents with heliotrope rash, Gottron’s papules, and proximal muscle weakness, with elevated CPK and necrosis on biopsy.

Medium|Clinical|

Most common type of juvenile idiopathic arthritis is?|Polyarticular|Enthesitis-related|Oligoarticular|Systemic-onset JIA|Oligoarticular|Oligoarticular JIA is the most common subtype, affecting ≤4 joints in the first 6 months of the disease.|Easy|General|

Juvenile idiopathic arthritis of systemic-onset variety, all are seen except?|High Fever|Salmon-colored rash|Hepatosplenomegaly|Uveitis in most patients|Uveitis in most patients|Uveitis is less common in systemic-onset JIA and is more associated with other JIA subtypes like oligoarticular JIA.|Medium|Clinical|

Characteristic feature of systemic-onset juvenile idiopathic arthritis?|Begins after 16 years of age|Uveitis is common|RA factor is negative|NSAIDs are contraindicated|RA factor is negative|Systemic-onset JIA is seronegative for rheumatoid factor and presents with systemic inflammation.|Medium|General|

Most common type of scleroderma in childhood is?|Diffuse systemic|Morphea|Bullous|Pansclerotic|Morphea|Morphea, a localized form of scleroderma, is the most common type seen in children, presenting as localized skin thickening without systemic involvement.|Easy|General|
